# JACOB BÖHME’S
## THE TEUTONIC PHILOSOPHER, <note>original: "Philosophi Teutonici"; Böhme was often referred to by this title to highlight his identity as a German mystic writing in the vernacular.</note>
# AURORA,
### Or the
# MORNING REDNESS
## in the Rising, <note>Also frequently translated into English as "The Day-Spring."</note>
### That is: The Root or Mother
### OF
## PHILOSOPHY, ASTROLOGY, and THEOLOGY,
### From a True Ground,
## Or Description of Nature,

How everything was and came to be in the beginning: How Nature and the Elements became creaturely; <term>creaturely: relating to the state or nature of a created being, as opposed to the uncreated Divine.</term> also of both qualities, Evil and Good / from whence all things have their origin / and how it will be at the end of this time: Also how the Kingdom of God and of Hell are constituted / and how humans work in each in a creaturely manner.

Everything set down with diligence from a true ground, in the knowledge of the Spirit in the surging <note>original: "wallen"; refers to a bubbling, boiling, or energetic movement of divine force.</note> of God. Revised anew, and corrected and improved with diligence according to the Author’s manuscript.

# PREFACE
->To all<-
## Righteous true Christians.

That this wretched, earthly life is nothing else than pure suffering <note>The original German "Creutz" literally means "cross," a common metaphor for the burdens and hardships a Christian must bear in imitation of Christ.</note> and tribulation; just consider this within yourself, my dear Christian. Does not your misery begin immediately in your tender youth, when you are barely raised under the disciplinarian’s rod, and having put away childhood, your years of youth are strained under <term>teachers</term> <note>original: "Præceptorn," a Latin-derived term for tutors or schoolmasters</note>, masters, and lords? If you reach your years of manhood, so too does your misery immediately double with the state of marriage—now with sickness, now with worry, now with this, now with that; it is all far too extensive to recount here. From one burden you come into another, from one unrest into another; one plague always opens the door for the next. Even if you remain single, you will likewise have your own share of distress. In short, the misery of human life is beyond description, all of which stems from the Fall of our first parents into sin, when **GOD** spoke to Adam:
> With sorrow you shall nourish yourself all the days of your life;

<summary>This preface introduces the reader to the inherent hardships of human life, tracing earthly misery from childhood through adulthood back to the biblical Fall of Man in Genesis.</summary>
<keywords>human misery, original sin, Adam, life's hardships, Christian endurance, marriage, youth, Genesis</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the biblical citation from the previous page, completing the reference to the curse of labor and sorrow following the Fall of Man. 4 Preface to all the Righteous

Genesis 3:17 <note>original: "Genes. 3. v. 17."</note>, a word which we must feel even until we reach the grave. Yet we wretched humans are so blind that, amidst all this unrest, we seek our rest; we hope for improvement where there is nothing to hope for; we constantly think, "soon, soon it shall be well," until death snatches us away, and—if we have not turned to the true rest in God—we are cast into eternal unrest. Therefore, my dear Christian, is it not better that we concern ourselves with eternal rest in good time—where we can be and remain in true rest for all eternity—rather than torturing and preoccupying ourselves with earthly, transitory matters? Let us seek our right, true rest where it is to be found, namely in God and in His holy Word. We do not need to run far to find it; we have the treasure within us, as the Lord Christ says in Luke 17:21 <note>original: "Luc. 17. v. 21."</note>: "Behold, the kingdom of God is within you." 

Whoever finds this rest cares nothing for all earthly unrest, nor for all earthly treasures; he sells all that he has and buys this pearl (Matthew 13:46) <note>original: "Matth. 13. v. 46."</note>. It is dearer to him than the whole world, with everything that is highly valued within it; he is entirely content in his soul, for even if he is the poorest person in this world, he is nonetheless the richest in God. Admittedly, in the beginning, eternal rest or the kingdom of God is like a grain of mustard seed (Matthew 13:31) <note>original: "Matth. 13. v. 31."</note>; however, over time it grows into a large tree, yet it is not recognizable in this world to anyone but **GOD** alone. For this rest and for this pearl, our late author <term>J. B.</term> <note>Jacob Böhme (1575–1624), the German mystic and cobbler whose spiritual writings are being introduced here.</note> felt a longing, [seeking] the same with

<meta>catchword: diligence (German: fleiß)</meta>

<summary>The author contrasts the restless, "blind" pursuit of earthly comfort with the eternal peace found within the soul, referencing Christ’s parables of the Mustard Seed and the Pearl of Great Price. It introduces Jacob Böhme as one who sought this spiritual rest above all earthly things.</summary>
<keywords>Eternal rest, Kingdom of God, Jacob Böhme, Pearl of Great Price, Mustard Seed, inwardness, spiritual wealth, Luke 17:21, Matthew 13</keywords>

Because he, through his writings and by Divine impulse, aimed to show the foundation to the disputing sects, and to remedy the vain Babylonian <note>Böhme used "Babylon" as a metaphor for the confusion, pride, and legalism of the established churches of his time.</note> opinions, and to rightly instruct everyone to learn to know God and themselves, Satan—who felt a significant blow to his anti-Christian kingdom through this—has not ceased to set himself against it with all his power in order to suppress such writings. ...intended. <note>This continues the thought from the previous page regarding the suppression of Böhme's writings.</note> 

First, his first book—the blessed **DAWN** <note>original: "MORGEN-RÖHTE." Usually known by its Latin title, *Aurora*, this was Böhme’s first work, written in 1612.</note> of the Seven Trumpets <note>A reference to the Book of Revelation; Böhme believed his era was the time of the "Seventh Trumpet," a period of final divine revelation.</note>—has come into public print according to the content of the manuscript <note>original: "Manuscripti." The editors are emphasizing that this edition is based on Böhme's original papers rather than unauthorized copies.</note> (because those previously in print do not match this one). We have wished hereby to recommend and place this heartfelt book into the hands of those who are right-minded, with the promise that, if we find that the children of God are served by it, we will make it our concern that the subsequent ones may come out in the same order as the author wrote them. For although some god-fearing people have previously brought those same writings into nine parts under certain titles and had them printed for the glory of **GOD**—and God will reward their precious labor in the Resurrection of the Just <note>A theological term for the biblical promise that the righteous will be raised to eternal life.</note>—they will nonetheless provide even more benefit if they are available in the order maintained by the **Holy Spirit** Himself.

Concerning the excellence of these writings, we truly know of no words to find that might sufficiently testify to them; for since the beginning of the world, such high things have never been so publicly described. You yourself will find this to be true when you now read this book through, and even more so if you earnestly continue into the following writings—to which we faithfully advise you, as well as to the diligent reading of Holy Scripture. Let nothing hinder you if, at first, your earthly reason <term>reason</term> <note>original: "Vernunfft." In Böhme's philosophy, "reason" refers to the limited, logical human intellect which cannot understand divine mysteries without the help of spiritual intuition.</note> is offended, or if Satan creates adverse thoughts within you, or if his children blaspheme against it; instead, just set

<meta>This page continues the "Preface of the Lovers," describing the divine inspiration of Jacob Böhme. -># Preface of the Lovers #<-

...put aside <note>The text continues here from the previous page, encouraging the reader to approach the work without bias.</note> all partisan judgment and breathe with a strong longing for the Spirit of God; then you will see great wonders in these writings and be convinced by heaven, earth, and all things—but most of all by your own conscience—that they are the pure Divine Truth, indeed the uncovered, bright face of Moses <note>A biblical reference to Exodus 34, where Moses veiled his face because it shone too brightly after speaking with God. The editors suggest Böhme reveals the "unveiled" truth.</note> which until now has been hidden by a veil. Indeed, one cannot find their equal in the world, nor can one demonstrate that it ever entered the heart of any highly learned person to express such high wonders as happens in these writings with a completely unusual and previously unheard-of tongue.

Beyond this, he also understood the innermost <term>language of essences or nature</term> <note>original: "Essentien-oder Natur-Sprache." This refers to Böhme's belief that there is a root language where the sound of a word reveals the inner character and "signature" of the thing named.</note>, according to which all words are formed and which is entirely unknown to the world. He likewise held in his knowledge the true <term>philosophy</term> <note>original: "Philosophiam."</note> and <term>magic</term> <note>original: "Magiam." In this context, "magic" refers to the spiritual power of the soul and the hidden workings of divine will in nature, rather than sorcery.</note>, along with the hidden <term>secret of the philosophers</term> <note>original: "secreto Philosophorum." This usually refers to the "Philosopher's Stone" of alchemy, which Böhme interpreted as a spiritual transformation or the finding of the divine center.</note> (to which all universities are blind). This is described in his writings far more highly than in all other books, and is moreover the very deepest core of <term>Holy Scripture</term> <note>original: "H. Schrifft."</note>, alongside the ungraspable, most holy, eternal birth of the Son of GOD, as much as a creature may endure to hear. Yet no school instructed him, except for the little reading and poor writing he learned in his youth, after which he soon had to become an apprentice in shoemaking. In this very lowly station, the <term>Holy Spirit</term> <note>original: "H. Geist."</note> nonetheless did not despise him, but rather filled him with wisdom beyond all nature—

From all of this, it now appears that these often-mentioned and profoundly wondrous writings are the work of the **Holy Spirit**, because He used such a humble and simple **layman** <note>Böhme was a shoemaker by trade with no university education, a point his followers often used to prove his "divine" rather than "scholarly" inspiration.</note> for them. This was done so that—had the author been from the high schools—one could not claim that the writings flowed from the subtle and pedantic thoughts of the learned. It is true that the highly learned **Doctor Balthasar Walter** <note>A physician and chemist who traveled extensively in the East; he was a close friend of Böhme and helped spread his work.</note> presented **40 Questions concerning the Soul** to him to answer; however, the answers to those very questions were already to be found in his previously written books. Thus, one cannot say that he drew any special thoughts from the questions of the learned. The beginning, middle, and end of his writing are consistent throughout and are to be regarded as **Divine** and not human writings. We wish to direct the reader to this fact, and hereby kindly request that he take our good intentions in the best possible light, allowing himself to be awakened by us to a longing for the **Wisdom of GOD** and to the righteous **fear of the LORD**, so that he might not—because now our **Bride-**

# PREFACE
#### Of the
## AUTHOR
### regarding this Book
### To the favorable Reader.

Favorable Reader / I compare all 
Philosophy, Astrology, and Theo-
logy <note>original: *Philosophiam, Astrologiam und Theologiam*</note> together with their mother <note>In Böhme’s thought, the "mother" of these three sciences is Nature or Divine Wisdom.</note> to a pre-
cious tree / which grows in a beautiful 
pleasure-garden <note>original: *Lust-Garten*; a common 17th-century term for a cultivated park or garden of delights.</note>.

Now the earth / in which the tree stands / 
constantly gives sap <note>original: *Safft*; refers to the vital life-force or nourishing essence.</note> to the tree / from which the tree has its 
living quality <term>quality: Böhme uses the word *Qualität* (from the Latin *qualitas*) to describe an inherent power, property, or driving force within a being or substance.</term>: but the tree in itself 
grows from the sap of the earth / and becomes large / 
and spreads itself out with its branches.

Now / just as the earth works with its power upon the 
tree / so that it may grow and increase / 
so the tree works constantly with its branches with 
all its might / so that it might always bring forth much good 
fruit.

But if the tree brings forth little fruit / 
and moreover very small / maggoty, and worm-eaten / the 
fault is not in the tree's will / that it 
deliberately desires to bear bad fruit / be-
cause it is a precious tree of good quality / but 
the fault is / that often great cold / heat / mil-
dew / caterpillars and vermin fall upon it / for

<meta>catchword: die</meta>

<summary>In this opening to his preface, Jacob Böhme uses an extended metaphor of a fruit tree in a garden to explain the relationship between God, Nature, and the three branches of knowledge (Philosophy, Astrology, and Theology). He argues that while the "tree" of knowledge is inherently good, its output can be corrupted by external spiritual and natural "pests."</summary>

For nature or the sap defends itself / until the trunk becomes quite dry / then it is hewn down and burned in the fire.

Even though evil clings to him—just as in nature the evil clings to the good—he can nonetheless overcome the evil. Mankind, however, threw themselves into this fierceness through the Fall of Adam and Eve <note>original: *Hevæ*; the Latin form of Eve.</note>, so that evil now clings to them; otherwise, their welling and impulse would have been in the good alone. But now it is in both, and it is as St. Paul says: “Do you not know that to whom you yield yourselves as servants in obedience, his servants you are whom you obey—whether of sin unto death, or of obedience to God unto righteousness?” (Romans 6:16). Since man has this impulse toward both, he may reach for whichever he desires; for he lives in this world between the two, and both qualities—evil and good—exist within him. Into whichever one man surges <note>original: *wallet*; Böhme uses this word to describe a soul "boiling" or "surging" with a particular spiritual intensity.</note>, with that power he is clothed, be it holy or hellish power. [Heaven]ly kingdom in nature has always and at all times struggled with one another / and stood in great labor / like a woman in childbirth. This is most clearly seen with Adam and Eve / for there a tree grew up in Paradise of both qualities <term>quality: *qualität*, Böhme’s term for the active forces or "source-springs" that drive all of reality.</term> / evil and good / there Adam and Eve were to be tested / whether they could remain in the good quality / in an angelic way and form. Therefore they also immediately received an animal-like nature and form / and ate of evil and good / and had to multiply and live in an animal-like way / and many a noble branch born of them was corrupted.

Afterwards one sees / how God worked in nature / when the holy fathers were born in the first world / such as Abel, Seth, Enosh, Cainan, Mahalalel, Jared, Enoch, Methuselah, Lamech, and the holy Noah: they proclaimed the name of the Lord to the world and preached repentance / for the Holy Spirit worked within them. ### Author’s Preface.

Therefore God said: *It repents me that I have made man* (Genesis 6) and he stirred up nature, so that all flesh died that lived on dry land, except for the root and stem, which remained standing: and with this he fertilized and prepared the wild tree, so that it should bear better fruit. But when the tree turned green again, it soon brought forth both good and evil fruits among the sons of Noah; there were soon once again mockers and despisers of God, and hardly one good branch grew on the tree that brought forth holy, good fruit; the other branches bore and brought forth the wild heathens.

When God saw that man was thus dead in his knowledge <term>knowledge: *Erkäntnis*, which for Böhme is not just intellectual learning, but a deep, living awareness of spiritual truth and God’s presence.</term>, he moved nature once more, and showed humanity how there was both evil and good within it, so that they might flee the evil and live in the good; and he let fire fall out of nature and set fire to Sodom and Gomorrah as a terrifying example to the world. But as human blindness took the upper hand, and they would not allow themselves to be taught by God’s Spirit, he gave them laws and teachings on how they should conduct themselves, and confirmed these with wonders and signs, so that the knowledge of the true God might not be extinguished.

But even with this, the light would not come to the light of day, for the darkness and wrathfulness <term>wrathfulness: *Grimmigkeit*, Böhme’s term for the harsh, fierce, or "bitter" force in nature that exists when the light of God's love is not felt.</term> in nature resisted, and its prince <note>Referring to the "Prince of this World," the personification of the dark or "hellish" principle.</note> ruled powerfully.

But when the tree of nature reached its middle age, it began and bore several mild, sweet

He describes the persistent struggle between the "light" of divine knowledge and the "wrathful" darkness that seeks to dominate human nature.</summary>

### Author’s Preface.

[was] both evil and good, so too was its fruit. Since, however, the people of this place had all grown out of wild nature, the tree grew above them all and became so large that its branches reached even into the precious land, beneath the holy tree.

This, however, was the reason the wild tree became so large: the peoples under the good tree all ran after the merchants who sold false wares, and they ate of the false fruit—which was also evil and good—and they imagined that they would become healthy through it. All the while, they left the holy, good, and powerful tree standing [neglected]. Meanwhile, they became ever blinder, more weary, and weaker, and they could not prevent the wild tree toward the North <note>original: *Mitternacht* (literally "midnight"). As noted previously, this signifies the North, associated here with the source of spiritual darkness or opposition.</note> from growing. For they were much too weary and weak; they saw well that it was a wild, evil tree, but they were too weary and weak and could not prevent its growth.

Had they not run after the merchants with the false wares and eaten of the false fruit, but had instead eaten from the precious tree, they would have become powerful enough to resist the wild tree. But because they lusted after <note>original: *nachhureten*. Literally "whored after," a strong biblical metaphor for spiritual infidelity or following false idols.</note> the wild nature in human trifles <term>human trifles: *Menschen-Tand*. This refers to superficial human inventions, traditions, or distractions that lack true spiritual substance.</term>, following the desires of their hearts in hypocrisy, so wild nature also ruled over them. Thus the wild tree grew high and far above them and corrupted them with its wild power. For the Prince of Fierceness <term>fierceness: *Grimmigkeit*. In Böhme’s theology, this is the quality of God’s wrath or the harsh, unrefined aspect of nature.</term> in nature gave the tree his power to corrupt the people who ate of the merchant's wild fruit.

This spiritual weakness allowed the "Prince of Fierceness" to exert power over humanity through nature.</summary>

### Author’s Preface.

Because they abandoned the Tree of Life and sought their own cleverness, like Mother Eve in Paradise, their own innate individual quality <term>quality: *qualität*. In this context, it refers to a specific driving property or inner nature that governs a person's character and desires.</term> ruled over them. Thus, they fell into such a powerful delusion, as *Saint Paul* says (2 Thessalonians 2:11). And the Prince of Wrath <note>original: *Grimmigkeit*. This is a central term for the author, referring to the fierce, dark aspect of nature or the divine that consumes when not balanced by love.</note> stirred up war and storm-winds from the wild tree toward the North <note>original: *Mitternacht* (literally "midnight"). This signifies the North, often associated in this period with darkness or spiritual opposition.</note> against the peoples who were not born from the wild tree; and they fell in their weariness and weakness before the tempest that came out of the wild tree.

And the merchant under the good tree acted hypocritically toward the peoples in the South <note>original: *Mittag* (literally "midday" or "noon"). This signifies the South.</note> and West <note>original: *Abend* (literally "evening"). This signifies the West.</note>, and toward the North. He highly praised his wares and deceived the simple-minded with cunning; he turned the clever ones into his own merchants and tradesmen so that they also profited from it. He continued until he brought things to the point where no one truly saw or recognized the holy tree anymore. Once he had gained the land as his own property, he had it proclaimed (2 Thessalonians 2): I am the trunk of the good tree, and I stand upon the root of the good tree, and I am grafted into the Tree of Life; buy my wares which I sell to you, and you will be healed from your wild birth and live forever. I have grown out of the root of the good tree, and I have the fruit of the holy tree in my power, and I sit upon the throne of divine power, and I have authority in heaven and on earth.

# Author’s Preface.

<sig>C 5</sig>

Favorable reader / this is a brief report of the two <term>qualities</term> <note>original: *qualitäten*. Böhme uses "quality" to describe the fundamental forces or "tastes" of reality (like sweetness, bitterness, or heat) that interact to create all matter and spirit.</note> in Nature from the beginning to the end / how two kingdoms arose from them / one Heavenly and one Hellish / and how in this time they surge and struggle against one another / and how it will go with them in the future.

Now I have given this book the name: **The Root or Mother of Philosophy, Astrology, and Theology** <note>original: *Philosophiæ, Astrologiæ und Theologiæ*</note>: so that you may know / what this book treats of / 1. Through **Philosophy** <note>original: *Philosophia*. To Böhme, this is "natural philosophy"—the study of the underlying spiritual mechanics of the physical world.</note> is treated / the divine power / what God is / and how in the Essence of God Nature / the Stars and the **Elements** <note>original: *Elementa*. These are the spiritual archetypes of the physical elements: Fire, Air, Water, and Earth.</note> are constituted / and from where all things have their origin / how Heaven and Earth are constituted / also angels / humans and devils / moreover Heaven and Hell / and all that is creaturely; also what both qualities in Nature are / from the true ground in the knowledge of the Spirit / in the impulse and surging of God.

2. Through **Astrology** <note>original: *Astrologia*. This refers to the study of the "astral" or "starry" spirit—the hidden cosmic influences that shape the physical behavior and temperaments of living things.</note> is treated / the powers of Nature / the Stars and the Elements / how all **creatures** <note>original: *Creaturen*. Used here to encompass all created beings, including animals and plants, as well as humans.</note> have come from them / and how they drive / govern and work in all things / and how evil and good is worked through them in humans and animals: from which it comes / that evil and good rule and exist in this world / also how the kingdoms of Hell and Heaven consist therein.

# Author’s Preface.

<header>Author’s Preface.</header>

...describe the course of all the stars, their positions or names, or how they annually have their **conjunction** <note>original: *conjunction*. An astrological alignment where two planets appear in the same place in the sky.</note> or **opposition** <note>original: *Gegen-schein*. When two celestial bodies are on opposite sides of the sky.</note> or **square** <note>original: *quadrat*. An astrological aspect where bodies are 90 degrees apart, often signaling tension.</note> and the like, or what they effect annually and hourly.

This has been learned through long experience by highly wise and clever, spirited men, through diligent observation, attention, deep reflection, and calculation. I have not studied or learned these things myself, and I leave such matters to the scholars to handle; rather, my intention is to write according to the Spirit and inner sense, and not according to outward observation.

3. Through **Theology** <note>original: *Theologia*</note> is treated the Kingdom of Christ, how it is constituted, how it is set in opposition to the kingdom of Hell, and also how it fights and struggles against the kingdom of Hell within Nature; and how humans, through faith and the Spirit, can overcome the kingdom of Hell and triumph in divine power, attaining eternal salvation and carrying it away as a victory in the struggle. Also, how humans, through the effect of the hellish <term>quality</term> <note>original: *qualität*. Böhme uses this to mean a fundamental force or characteristic of being.</note>, throw themselves into corruption, and finally, how it will turn out for both in the end.

The primary title: **The Aurora** or **The Day-Spring in the Rising** <note>original: *Morgenröte im Auffgang*. This refers to the breaking of spiritual light through the darkness of human ignorance.</note> is a secret **mystery** <note>original: *Mysterium*</note>, hidden from the clever and wise of this world, which they themselves shall soon be forced to experience. But to those who read this book in simplicity, with a longing for the Holy Spirit, and who place their hope in God alone, it will not be a mystery, but a manifest knowledge.

# Author’s Preface. 43

<header>Author’s Preface. 43</header>

I do not wish to explain this title, but rather leave it to the judgment of the impartial reader who struggles in this world within the good <term>quality</term> <note>Böhme uses "quality" (original: *qualität*) to describe the fundamental energetic properties or "forces" of nature and divinity.</note>.

Now, when **Master Know-it-all** <note>original: *Meister Klügling*. A mocking term Böhme uses for self-important scholars who rely on intellectual pride rather than spiritual insight.</note>, who is characterized by the wrathful <term>quality</term>, comes across this book, he will take the opposing side, just as the kingdoms of Heaven and Hell surge and exist against one another. First, he will say I have climbed far too high into the **Godhead** <note>original: *Gottheit*. The essential essence of God.</note> and that such a thing is not fitting for me. Second, he will say that I boast of the **Holy Spirit**, and that I must therefore live accordingly and prove such claims with **miracles**. Third, he will say I do this out of a desire for fame. Fourth, he will say I am not learned enough for this. Fifth, the great **simplicity** <note>original: *Einfalt*. In Böhme's context, this refers to a lack of formal academic training and a humble, childlike spirit.</note> of the author will greatly offend him, as is the custom of the world—to look only upon what is high and to be offended by simplicity.

Against these biased "know-it-alls," I will set the **Patriarchs** <note>original: *Alt-Vätter*. Literally "Old Fathers."</note> of the first world; they too were but lowly, despised people, against whom the world and the Devil raged and stormed. As in the time of **Enoch** <note>original: *Henoch*</note>, when the holy fathers first powerfully preached the name of the Lord; they did not ascend bodily into heaven and see everything with their eyes, but rather the Holy Spirit revealed itself within their spirit. Later, one sees this in the other world among the holy fathers, patriarchs, and prophets; they were all merely simple people, and some were but **shepherds** <note>original: *Viehhirten*. Literally "cattle-herds."</note>. Even when the **Messiah Christ**, the hero of the struggle, became a man in nature—even though he was a Prince and King

It is not an empty word that stands in **Genesis chap. 1, v. 21**: <note>The text cites verse 21, though the quote "God created man in his image" is found in Genesis 1:27.</note> *God created man in his image, yes, in the image of God he created him.* For it has precisely this meaning: that he has been made out of the entire essence of the **Deity** <term>Deity</term> <note>original: *Gottheit*. Here you shall see the true **ground** <term>ground</term> <note>original: *Grund*. A mystical term for the underlying foundation or essence of being.</note> of the **Deity** <note>original: *Gottheit*.</note>, and how it was one **essence** <term>essence</term> <note>original: *Wesen*. Can mean "being," "substance," or "nature."</note> before the times of the world; also how the holy angels were created, and from what: also of the terrible fall of Lucifer together with his legions; also from what heaven, earth, stars, and **elements** <note>original: *Elementa*.</note> were made, as well as the metals and stones in the earth, and all **creatures** <note>original: *Creaturen*.</note>: how the birth of life and the corporeality of all things came to be: also what the true heaven is, where **GOD** and his saints dwell, and what the wrath of God and the hellish fire are, and how everything became **ignitable** <note>original: *anzündlich*. A technical term in this philosophy referring to how the potential for either divine light or wrathful fire can be awakened or "set ablaze" within a being.</note>: In summary, what or how the **Essence of all essences** <note>original: *Wesen aller wesen*. A description of God as the ultimate reality underlying all individual existences.</note> is.

The first seven chapters treat quite **simply** <note>original: *schlecht*. In the German of this period, this meant "plainly" or "directly" rather than "poorly."</note> and comprehensibly of the essence of God and angels

with **parables** <note>original: *Gleichnissen*. The author uses the Latin term to refer to the various appearances or categories of spiritual manifestation.</note> is often repeated and described ever more deeply / for the sake of the reader’s—and even my own—slow comprehension.

With this, I commend the reader into the gentle and holy love of God.

# Chapter 1.

## On the Investigation of the Divine Being in Nature.

### Concerning the Two Qualities.

**A**lthough flesh and blood cannot grasp the **Divine Being** <note>original: *Göttliche Wesen*. The essential nature, substance, or "beingness" of God.</note> / but rather the spirit / when it is enlightened and kindled by God : yet if one wishes to speak of God, of what God is / then one must diligently consider the powers in nature / as well as the entire creation / heaven and earth / as well as the stars and **elements** <note>original: *Elementa*. In the 17th century, these were the four building blocks of the universe: Earth, Water, Air, and Fire.</note> / and the creatures / which have come forth from them / as well as the holy angels / devils and humans / also heaven and hell.

In such contemplation, one finds two **qualities** <term>qualities</term> <note>original: *Qualitäten*. For Böhme, a 'quality' is not just a passive trait, but an active, driving force or "source-springing" power within nature.</note>; a good one and an evil one / which in this world are in all powers / in stars and elements / as well as in all creatures, within one another / like one single thing : and no creature in the flesh exists in the natural life / unless it has both qualities within itself. Here one must now consider / what the word quality means or is. Quality is the mobility / the **bubbling** <note>original: *quallen*. A wordplay unique to Böhme, linking "quality" to the "welling up" or "springing forth" of a fountain (*Quelle*).</note> or the driving of a thing / such as heat which burns / consumes, and drives everything / that comes into it / that is not of its own—

<summary>Böhme begins his treatise by arguing that God's nature can be understood by studying the dual "qualities" of good and evil that permeate all of creation, from the elements to the angels.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Being, Nature, Creation, Two Qualities, Elements, Mobility, Welling-forth, Jacob Böhme</keywords>

->The Day-Spring at its Rising.<-
<note>original: *Morgenröhte im Auffgang*. This is the title of the work, often known in Latin as *Aurora*.</note>

[is] created <note>This completes the word "Qualitschafft" from the previous page, Böhme’s term for the active, working nature or "working-property" of a quality.</note>. On the other hand, it enlightens and warms everything that is cold, wet, and dark / and makes the soft hard. It has, however, two species <note>original Latin: *species*. Here meaning types, forms, or distinct appearances.</note> within itself / namely the Light and the **Fierceness** <term>fierceness</term> <note>original: *Grimmigkeit*. A central concept for Böhme, referring to a wrathful, consuming, or harsh energy that acts as the necessary "friction" or drive for life, but can become destructive if not tempered by Light.</note>: concerning which is to be noted. The Light or the Heart of the Heat / is in itself a lovely / joy-filled sight / a power of life / an enlightenment and sight of a thing that is far off / and is a piece or source of the heavenly Kingdom of Joy. For it makes everything in this world living and mobile / all flesh / as well as trees / foliage and grass grows in this world by the power of the Light / and has its life therein / as in that which is good. Conversely, it has within itself **Fierceness**, such that it burns / consumes and destroys; this same fierceness springs / drives and raises itself in the Light / and makes the Light mobile / wrestling and struggling with one another in its twofold source as one thing: it is indeed one thing: but it has a twofold source. The Light exists in **GOD** without Heat / but in nature it does not; for in nature all qualities are in one another like one quality / according to the manner and way / how **GOD** is all / and how from Him everything comes and goes forth / **GOD** is the Heart or Wellspring of nature / from Him everything originates.

Now the Heat rules in all powers in nature / and warms everything / and is a source in all / otherwise if that were not so / then the water would be much too cold / and the earth would freeze stiff / also there would be no air.

The Heat rules in all / in trees / herbs and

and grass / and makes the water mobile / so that through the water’s source, herb and grass grow out of the earth / therefore it is called a quality, <note>original: *Qualität*. For Böhme, a "quality" is not just a description, but an active, "welling up" power or property of being.</note> because it springs up in all things and raises everything up.

The Light, however, within the Heat gives power to all qualities / so that everything becomes lovely and full of wonder: Heat without Light is of no use to the other qualities / but is instead a destruction of the good / an evil source / for it destroys everything in the heat’s **fierceness** <term>fierceness</term> <note>original: *Grimmigkeit*. ### Of the Working of Cold.
<note>original: *Von der Kälte qualificirung*. Böhme uses the word "qualificirung" to describe the active process of a quality working or manifesting its power within nature.</note>

Cold is also a quality / just like heat / it works within all creatures / whatever has come out of nature / and in everything that moves therein / in humans / animals / birds / fishes / worms / foliage and grass / and it is set in opposition to heat / and works within the same / as if they were one thing / but it resists the heat’s **fierceness** / and stills the heat.

But this also has two species <note>original Latin: *species*. Here meaning types, forms, or distinct modes of appearance.</note> within itself / of which it should be noted / namely that it makes the heat gentler and makes everything fine and lovely / and is in all creatures a quality of life / for no creature can exist without the cold / for it is a springing / driving mobility in all things.

The other *species* <note>original: *species*. Latin for "kind" or "type."</note> is fierceness; for when it gains power, it presses everything down and spoils everything just like heat; no life can exist within it if heat does not restrain it. The fierceness of the cold is a destruction of all life and a house of death, just as the fierceness of the heat is also.

### Of the Working of the Air and the Water.
<note>original: *Von der Lufft und des Wassers qualificirung*. Böhme uses "qualificirung" to describe the active process or functioning of these elements within the natural order.</note>

The air has its origin from heat and cold; for heat and cold drive powerfully away from themselves and fill everything, out of which comes a living and weaving <note>original: *webende*. Böhme frequently uses "weaving" to describe the interlacing, vibrating activity of natural forces as they interact.</note> movement. When the cold softens the heat, then the quality of both becomes thin, and the bitter quality <term>bitter quality</term> <note>One of Böhme's foundational properties of nature, representing the contracting force that draws spirit into a more tangible or material form.</note> contracts it so that it becomes droplets; the air, however, has its origin and greatest movement from heat, and the water from cold.

Now the two qualities constantly wrestle with one another: heat consumes the water, and cold constrains the air. Yet the air is a cause and spirit of all life and all movement in this world, whether in the flesh or in everything that grows out of the earth; thus everything has its life from the air, and nothing that moves in this world can exist without the air.

The water also wells up in all living and weaving things in this world; in the water consists the body of all things, and in the air the spirit, whether in the flesh or in the plants out of the earth: and both of these come from the

<summary>Böhme explains how the conflict and tempering between Heat and Cold produce Air (the spirit of life) and Water (the substance of the body). He emphasizes that life cannot exist in "fierceness" alone, but requires a balance of these moving qualities.</summary>
<keywords>fierceness, air, water, heat, cold, spirit, body, weaving movement, qualities, Jakob Böhme</keywords>

Heat and cold / and they function <note>original: *qualificiret*. This term describes the active, living process of natural forces interacting.</note> together as one thing.

Now, however, in these two qualities, two distinct types <note>original: *species*. Latin for "kind" or "type."</note> are also to be noted, namely the living and the deadly effect. The air is a living quality when it is gentle within a thing, and the Holy Spirit <note>original: *H. Geist*</note> reigns in the gentleness of the air, and all creatures are joyful within it. But it also has fierceness <term>fierceness</term> <note>original: *Grimmigkeit*. In Böhme’s cosmology, this is the "wrathful" or harsh side of nature that provides the necessary friction for life but can also destroy.</note> within it, so that it kills and spoils through its cruel elevation. This functioning, however, takes its origin from that fierce elevation, so that it wells up and drives in everything; from this, life has its origin and existence; therefore, both must be present in this life.

The water also has a fierce, deadly quality <note>original: *quall*. A term Böhme uses to describe the "welling source" or "fountain" of a particular power.</note> within itself, for it kills and consumes: furthermore, everything living and weaving <note>original: *webende*. Böhme uses this to describe the vibrant, interlacing movement of the spirit within matter.</note> must rot and perish in the water.

Thus, heat and cold are a cause and origin of the water and the air, in which everything works and exists; all life and mobility resides therein, about which I intend to write clearly regarding the creation <note>original: *erschöpffung*. Here meaning the "drawing forth" or formative production of the stars.</note> of the stars.

### Of the Harsh or Salty Quality.

The salty quality is a good temperance <note>In Böhme’s view, a "temperance" is a balancing force that keeps other qualities from becoming too dominant or extreme.</note> within the bitter / sweet, and sour / and makes everything quite lovely; it prevents the rising of the bitter quality / as well as the sweet and sour / so that they do not ignite: it is a sharp quality / a delight of the taste / a source of life and joys. Conversely, it also contains within it fierceness and destruction / when it is ignited in the fire / it brings forth a hard / tearing / stony nature / a fierce source / a corruption of life / from which the stone <note>Böhme is likely referring to medical conditions like kidney or bladder stones, which he views as a spiritual imbalance manifested in the body.</note> grows in the flesh / from which the flesh suffers great torment. But if it is ignited in the water / it brings forth in the flesh evil scabs / ulcers / the French disease <note>original: *Franzosen*. A common historical term for syphilis, then believed to be caused by an imbalance of internal "qualities."</note> / the itch, and leprosy / and a house of sorrow of death / a misery and forgetting of all good.

## Chapter 2.
### Instruction on how one should contemplate the Divine and Natural Being.

All of this / as told above / is called "quality" because everything in the depth above the earth / on the earth / and in the earth qualifies <note>original: *qualificiret*. For Böhme, this verb describes how different spiritual forces interact, mix, and operate within one another to create reality.</note> together as one thing / and yet has many kinds of power and effect / but only one Mother / from which all things come and well forth: and all creatures are made from these qualities.

<summary>Böhme concludes his description of the Salty Quality, showing how it balances other forces but causes physical diseases like syphilis and stones when corrupted, then begins Chapter 2 by defining "quality" as the unified interaction of all natural forces.</summary>

[quali]ties <note>The text continues from the word "qualities" on the previous page.</note> were made and originated, and live within them as in their mother; also the earth and stones have their origin from there, and everything that grows from the earth lives and wells up from the power of these qualities; this no rational person can deny.

This twofold source, evil and good in all things, all originates from the stars; for as the creatures on earth are in their quality, so also are the stars. For through this twofold source everything has its great mobility—its running, racing, welling, driving, and growing. For gentleness in nature is a quiet rest, but fierceness <note>original: *Grimmigkeit*. In Böhme’s thought, this "fierceness" or "wrath" is the necessary friction or energy that causes movement and growth, though it can become destructive if unbalanced.</note> in all powers makes everything mobile, running, and racing, and additionally generative. For the driving qualities bring a desire into all creatures for evil and for good, so that everything desires one another, mingles, increases, decreases, becomes beautiful, perishes, loves, and hates.

In all creatures in this world there is a good and an evil will and source: in humans, beasts, birds, fish, worms, as well as in all that is in gold, in silver, tin, copper, iron, steel, wood, herbs, leaves, and grass; as well as in the earth, in stones, in water, and in everything that can be investigated.

There is nothing in nature that does not have good and evil within it; everything seethes and lives in this twofold impulse, whatever it may be—except for the holy angels and the fierce devils, for they are separated <note>original: *entschieden*. Meaning the angels and devils are no longer part of the "mixed" world of our reality, but have been fixed in their respective realms of pure light or pure wrath.</note>; and each lives, qualifies <note>original: *qualificiret*. A term Böhme uses to describe how a spiritual power acts, mixes, and exerts its influence.</note>, and rules in its own—

quality. <note>The text continues from the previous page's discussion on the nature of "qualities."</note> The holy angels live and interact <note>original: *qualificiren*. In Böhme's specialized vocabulary, this means to live, act, and be composed of specific spiritual "qualities."</note> in the light in the good quality, wherein the Holy Spirit rules: the devils live and rule in the fierce quality, in the quality of fierceness and wrath or destruction.

But they, both evil and good angels, were made from the qualities of Nature, from which all things have come to be; only the interaction within them is unequal.

The holy angels live in the power of the gentleness of the Light and the kingdom of joy; and the devils live in the power of the rising or elevating source of fierceness, terror, and darkness, and cannot grasp the light, into which state they thrust themselves through their own self-exaltation, as I will write hereafter concerning the Creation.

But if you will not believe that in this world everything originates from the stars, then I will prove it to you; provided you are not a blockhead <note>original: *Klotz*. Literally a log or block of wood; used here to mean someone dull-witted or unthinking.</note> and have a little reason, then take note of what follows:

First, look at the sun; it is the heart or the king of all the stars, and gives light to all stars from sunrise to sunset, and enlightens everything, and warms everything; everything lives and grows in its power, and additionally, the joy of all creatures resides in its power.

Now, if the same were taken away, it would become entirely dark and cold; also, no fruit would grow, nor would man or beast be able to multiply, for the heat would be extinguished, and the seed in everything would become cold and frozen.

### Of the Sun’s Quality.

If you wish to be a <term>philosopher</term> <note>original: *Philosophus*. In Böhme's time, this referred to a "natural philosopher" or someone seeking the deep, hidden truths of the universe.</note> and a <term>naturalist</term> <note>original: *Naturkündiger*. One who is knowledgeable about the inner workings and secrets of nature.</note>, and to search out God’s being in nature and how all this is constituted, then ask God for His Holy Spirit, that He might enlighten you with the same.

For in your flesh and blood you cannot grasp such things; even if you read of it, it is only a mist or a conceit <note>original: *Dünckel*. A superficial opinion or a delusion of knowledge.</note> before your eyes. Only in the Holy Spirit—who is in God and also in all of nature, upon which all things were made—can you search even into the whole body of God, which is nature, as well as into the holy <term>Trinity</term> <note>original: *Trinität*. The Christian concept of the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit.</note>. For the Holy Spirit goes out from the holy <term>Trinity</term> and rules in the whole body of God—that is, in the whole of nature.

Just as the spirit of a man rules in the whole body in all the veins and fills the whole man, so also the Holy Spirit fills all of nature; He is the heart of nature and rules in the good qualities in all things. Now, if you have Him within you, so that He enlightens and fills your spirit, then you will understand what is written hereafter; but if not, then it will happen to you as it did to the wise heathens <note>original: *Heyden*. Ancient non-Christian philosophers, such as the Greeks and Romans.</note>, who stared blindly at <note>original: *vergaffeten*. To be transfixed by outward appearances while missing the inner essence.</note> the creation and wanted to search it out through their own reason; in their imaginings they came even before God’s countenance, yet they could not see it, and were stone-blind in the knowledge of God. Just as the children of Israel in the wilderness could not [look upon] the face of Moses

# The Day-Spring at its Rising. 65

The head contains within itself the five senses, namely: seeing, hearing, smelling, tasting, and feeling. Within these, the stars and elements qualify <term>qualify: from the German *qualificiren*, meaning the way distinct forces or spirits interact, intermingle, and influence one another's properties.</term>, and therein arises the sidereal <note>original: *Siderische*; pertaining to the stars or constellations</note> star-spirit and nature-spirit in humans and animals. In these, both good and evil well up, for the head is a house of the stars. The stars take such power from heaven that they can create a living and moving spirit within the flesh of humans and animals. Just as the movement of heaven makes the stars mobile, so too the head makes the body mobile.

Here, now, open the eyes of your spirit and behold God your Creator. Here now is the question: From whence then does heaven have or take such power, that it creates such mobility in nature?

At this point, you must look above and beyond nature, into the light-holy, triumphant, divine power; into the unchangeable Holy Trinity, which is a triumphant, welling <note>original: *quallend*; suggesting a bubbling or pulsating fountain of life</note>, and mobile Being, and all powers are within it, just as they are in nature. For that is the eternal Mother of Nature, from which heaven, earth, stars, elements, angels, devils, humans, animals, and all things have come to be, and in which all things consist.

When one names heaven and earth, stars and elements, and all that is within them, and all that is above all heavens, one names herewith the whole God, who has made himself creaturely <note>original: *creatürlich*; referring to God's manifestation in the form of created beings or physical existence</note> in this aforementioned being, through the power that proceeds from Him.

# The Day-Spring at its Rising. 66

But God in His Trinity is unchangeable; rather, everything that is in heaven and on earth and above the earth has its source and origin from the Power that goes out from God.

You must not think that, for this reason, evil and good well up or exist within God; rather, God Himself is the Good, and also takes His name from "the Good" <note>In German, the word for God (*Gott*) and the word for good (*gut*) are closely related, a point Böhme uses to emphasize God's essential nature.</note>, the triumphant, eternal Joy. All powers alone proceed out of Him—those which you can research in nature and which are in all things.

Now you might say: "There is indeed evil and good in nature; since all things come from God, then surely evil must also come from God?"

Behold, a human being has within himself a gall <note>original: *Galle*; bile. In Böhme’s system, the gall is the "bitter" quality that provides the friction or "sting" necessary for life and movement.</note>, which is poison; yet he cannot live without the gall, for the gall makes the <term>Sidereal Spirits: from the Latin *sidereus* (starry); these are the "star-spirits" or astral forces within the human body that govern movement, emotion, and the senses.</term> mobile, full of joy, triumphant, or laughing; for it is a source of joy. However, if it ignites in one element <note>meaning if the balance of the four humors or elements is disturbed</note>, it corrupts the whole person, for the wrath in the sidereal spirits comes from the gall.

That is to say: when the gall rises and runs to the heart, it ignites the element of Fire, and the Fire ignites the sidereal spirits which rule in the blood and veins within the element of Water; then the whole body trembles with the wrath and poison of the gall. Even such a source does joy have, and also from this very substance, just as wrath does. That is: when the gall ignites in the loving or sweet quality—in that which is dear to a person—then the whole body trembles for joy—

And although it is written in Moses (Exodus 20, Deuteronomy 4:24): *I am an angry, jealous God*, this does not mean that God becomes angry within Himself, or that a **fire of wrath** arises within the Holy Trinity.

If God were to become angry within Himself, all of nature would burn; this will happen once on the **Last Day** <note>The Day of Judgment.</note> within nature, and not in God. In God, however, the **triumphing joy** shall burn, just as it has never been otherwise from eternity, nor shall it ever become otherwise.

## Chapter 3.

### Concerning the Highly Blessed, Triumphing, Holy, Holy, Holy Trinity: God the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit, One GOD.

<summary>Böhme describes a hierarchical chain of creation proceeding from God's power through the heavens and stars down to the earth, then announces a deeper investigation into the nature of the Holy Trinity.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the transition from the general introduction of the work's purpose to a specific theological exploration of the nature of the Trinity. The previous page concluded by announcing Chapter 3, which focuses on God the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit.</meta>

-># The Day-Spring at its Rising. 69<-

Kind reader, I wish to faithfully warn you here that you should let go of your **conceit** <note>original: *Dünckel*; refers to intellectual arrogance or a high opinion of one's own reason.</note> and not gaze blindly after **pagan wisdom** <note>Likely referring to Aristotelian or scholastic philosophy which Böhme felt obscured spiritual truth.</note>, nor take offense at the **simplicity of the author**; for this work is not a product of his **reason** but of the impulse of the **Spirit**. See to it only that you have the **Holy Spirit**, who proceeds from God, within your own spirit; He will lead you into all truth and reveal Himself to you. Then, in His light and power, you will see clearly even into the **Holy Trinity** and understand what is written hereafter.

### Concerning God the Father.

When our Savior Jesus Christ taught his disciples to pray, he said: "When you wish to pray, say: Our Father, who art in heaven." Matthew 6. This does not have the meaning that heaven could contain or encompass the Father, for heaven itself is made out of divine power.

For Christ says: "My Father is greater than all." John 10:29. And in the prophet, God says: "Heaven is my throne, and the earth is my footstool." Isaiah 66. Also: "What house will you build for me? I encompass the heaven with a span <note>A 'span' is the distance from the tip of the thumb to the tip of the little finger; a metaphor for God's immense scale.</note>, and the ground of the earth with a measure <note>original: *Dreylinge*; an archaic term for a small dry measure, used here to suggest that the entire earth is but a handful to God.</note>." Isaiah 40:12. Also: "In Jacob I will dwell, and Israel shall be my tent." Psalm 135:4; Sirach 24:13.

But that Christ calls his Father a **heavenly Father**, he means thereby, that his

-># The Day-Spring at its Rising. 70<-

The Father's brilliance and power appear quite pure, bright, and clear in heaven; and above the circle or boundary <note>original: *Circk oder Schluß*; Böhme refers to the perceived limit of the physical sky or the celestial sphere.</note> that we see there with our eyes, which we call heaven, appears the entire Triumphant Holy Trinity: Father, Son, and Holy Spirit.

Christ also distinguishes his heavenly Father from the Father of Nature, who consists of the stars and elements <note>original: *Sterne und Elementa*; in Böhme’s worldview, the celestial bodies and the four elements are the "parents" of the physical body.</note>; these are our natural father, out of which we are made, and in whose impulse <note>original: *trieb*; the driving force or instinctual influence of the material world.</note> we live here in this world, and from whom we take our food and nourishment.

He is our heavenly Father because our soul constantly yearns for him and desires him; indeed, it thirsts and hungers after him. The body hungers and thirsts after the Father of Nature, which are the stars and elements, and that same father also feeds and gives it drink. But the soul thirsts after the heavenly holy Father, and he also feeds and gives it drink with his Holy Spirit and <term>source of joy (Freuden-quall): Böhme uses the word *quall* not to mean "agony" (as in modern German *Qual*), but to describe the "quelling" or "welling up" of life-force and qualities.</term>.

But now we do not have two fathers, but only one; heaven is made out of his power, and the stars out of his wisdom, which is in him and which proceeds from him.

Now, when one considers the whole of nature and its attributes, one sees the Father: when one gazes upon the heaven and the sta-

-rein <note>This completes the word from the previous page, likely "where-in" or "where-by."</note>, then his eternal power and wisdom are seen. Just as many stars as stand under the heavens—which are yet innumerable and incomprehensible to human reason, and some even invisible—just so many and manifold are the power and wisdom of God the Father.

Now, every star in the sky has a different power and **quality** <term>quality: Böhme uses the word *Qualität* (or *Qual*) to describe a "source-property" or a specific driving force behind how something exists or feels.</term> than the next, which also creates so many different distinctions in and among the creatures on Earth, and in the whole of creation. But now, all powers that exist in nature originate from God the Father: such as light, heat, cold, air, water, and all the powers of the earth—bitter, sour, sweet, sharp <note>original: *herbe*; often translated as "astringent" in Böhme's specialized vocabulary, representing the contractive force of nature.</note>, hard, soft, and that which cannot be recounted—all of this has its outflowing from the Father.

If one wishes to compare the Father with something, however, one must compare him to the round sphere of the heavens. You must not think that each power that is in the Father stands in a specific part or place within the Father, like the stars in the sky. No; rather, the Spirit shows that all powers in the Father are within one another, as **One Power**, just as one has an image of this in the prophet Ezekiel in the 1st Chapter. There, he sees the Lord in the spirit and in a figure like a wheel, where four wheels are within each other, and all four were like one another; and when they went, they went straight forward; toward whichever side the wind went, there all four went forward, and none needed to turn around. So also is God the Father; for all powers in the Father are in-

-># The Day-Spring at its Rising. 72<-

...within one another like **One Power**; and all powers subsist in the Father in an unfathomable light and clarity.

You must not think that God in heaven and above the heaven stands and moves like a power and **quality** <term>quality: Böhme uses the word *Qualität* (from the Latin *qualis*) to describe a "source-property" or a specific driving force behind how something exists or feels.</term> that has no reason or knowledge within it—like the sun, which runs its course and pours out heat and light, whether it brings harm or benefit to the earth and its creatures, which would indeed happen if the other planets and stars did not restrain it. No, the Father is not like that; rather, He is an almighty, all-wise, all-knowing, all-seeing, all-hearing, all-smelling, all-feeling, all-tasting GOD, who is in Himself gentle, friendly, lovely, merciful, and joyful—indeed, He is Joy itself.

He is, moreover, unchangeable from eternity to eternity; He has never changed in His essence, nor will He change throughout all eternity. He has come from nothing, nor was He born; rather, He is Himself All-in-all in eternity, and everything that exists has come from His power, which goes out from Him. Nature and all creatures have come from His power, which has gone out from Him from eternity: no creature—not even an angel in heaven—can fathom His width, height, and depth; rather, the angels live in the Father’s power quite gently and joyfully, and sing forever in the Father’s power.

The Day-Spring at its Rising. 73

## Concerning God the Son.

If one now wishes to see GOD the Son, one must once again look at natural things; otherwise, I cannot write about Him. The spirit sees Him well, but one cannot speak <gloss>speak</gloss> or write of it, for the divine essence consists in power that cannot be written or spoken. Therefore, we must use parables <note>Böhme uses "Gleichnisse," meaning metaphors, parables, or analogies from the natural world to explain the spiritual.</note> for ourselves when we want to speak of God, for in this world we live in fragments <term>fragments: A reference to the biblical concept of "knowing in part" (1 Corinthians 13), suggesting our mortal understanding is piecemeal rather than whole.</term> and have been made out of fragments. Therefore, I wish to summon <note>original: "citiret," from the Latin *citare* (to summon or cite)</note> the reader to that other life; there I will speak more properly and clearly with him about this high article of faith. In the meantime, let the dear reader look to the sense of the spirit; then it will not fail, and he will also receive a little spark of power from it, if only he hungers for it.

->Now observe.<-

The Turks and heathens say that God has no Son. Here, open your eyes wide and do not make yourselves stone-blind, and you shall see the Son.

The Father is all, and all power subsists in the Father; He is the beginning and the end of all things, and outside of Him there is nothing; and everything that has come to be has come from the Father. For before the beginning of the creation of the creatures, there was nothing but God alone. And where there is now nothing, nothing comes from it; every thing must have a cause or root, otherwise nothing becomes. But now you must not think that the Son is another God

...be as the Father: you must also not think that the Son is outside the Father, or is a separate part, as when two men stand next to each other, where one does not encompass the other. No, the relationship between the Father and the Son does not have such a physical substance <note>Böhme is warning against imagining the Trinity as separate physical bodies.</note>; for the Father is not an image to be compared with something, but the Father is the fountain-source of all powers, and all powers are in one another, like One power; therefore He is also called One God. Otherwise, if His powers were divided, He would not be almighty; but now He is the self-subsistent, almighty, and all-powerful God.

The Son, however, is the Heart in the Father. All powers that are in the Father are the Father's own property, and the Son is the Heart or the kernel in all the powers in the whole Father; but He is the cause of the welling joys in all the powers in the whole Father. From the Son, who is the Father's Heart in all His powers, rises up eternal heavenly joy, and there wells up in all the powers of the Father such a joy as no eye has seen and no ear has heard, and has never entered into the heart of any man, as *St. Paul* says in *1 Corinthians 2:9*.

But if a person here on Earth is enlightened with the Holy Spirit <note>original: "H. Geist"</note> from the fountain-source of *Jesus Christ*, so that the spirits of nature (which represent the Father) are kindled, then such a joy rises in his heart into all his veins that the entire body trembles, and the *animal* <term>animal spirit: from the Latin "anima" (soul); this refers to the vital spirit or soul-force that animates the physical body, rather than "animalistic" in a modern sense.</term> spirit triumphs, as if it were in the holy *Tri-* <note>The text cuts off here to be completed by the next page's catchword: "nitåt," forming "Trinität" or Trinity.</note>

### Here I must now write in parables.

Here I will show you a parable <term>parable: or "similitude" (Gleichnis); Böhme uses natural phenomena as mirrors to explain divine mysteries.</term> in nature / as to how the holy being is in the **Holy Trinity**. Look at the sky / it is a round globe / and has neither beginning nor end / but the beginning and the end are everywhere / wherever you look at it : so also is **God** in and above the heavens / who has neither beginning nor end. Now look further at the circle of stars / they signify the **Father's** manifold power and wisdom / and they were also made out of the **Father's** power and wisdom. Now the heavens / the stars / and the whole depth between the stars / together with the Earth / signify the **Father** : and the **seven planets** signify the **seven spirits of God** / or the **Princes of the Angels** / among whom **Lord Lucifer** was also one before his fall / who were all made out of the **Father** in the beginning of the **Creation** of the angels / before the time of the world. Now mark / the **Sun** moves in the midst of the depth between the stars in the round circle / and it is the **heart of the stars** / and gives all stars light and power / and tempers <note>Balances or moderates.</note> the power of all stars /

...th, so that everything becomes lovely and joyful: it also illuminates the heavens, the stars, and the depth above the earth, and works in all things that are in this world; it is the queen and the **heart** of all things in this world, and it truly signifies **God the Son**.

For just as the sun stands in the middle between the stars and the earth, illuminating all **powers** <term>powers: (Kräfte); in Böhme's worldview, these are the active spiritual qualities or energies within nature and the divine.</term>, and is the light and heart of all powers, and all joy in this world; moreover, all beauty and loveliness resides in the sun’s light and power:

If one could take away the earth—which signifies the house of misery or of hell—then the entire depth would be completely light in one place as in another: so also is the entire depth in the **Father** completely light in every place, from the radiance of the **Son of God**. 

And just as the sun is an independent creature, power, or light, which does not shine *out of* all creatures, but *into* all creatures—and all creatures rejoice in its power—so also is the **Son** in the **Father** an independent person; He illuminates every power in the Father, and is the Father’s joy or heart in His **center** <note>original: "centro"; Latin for center or midpoint.</note> or midst.

## Here, observe the great mysteries of God!

The sun is born or made from all the stars, and is the light taken from the whole of nature; it shines back into the entire nature of this world and is bound with the other stars as if it were one single star with all the others.

So too is the **Son of God** eternally born—and not made—from all the **powers** <term>powers: (Kräfte); the active spiritual qualities or energies within the divine essence.</term> of His **Father**. He is the **Heart** and the **Radiance** from all the powers of His heavenly Father, a self-subsisting person, the <term>center</term> <note>original: "Centrum"; the focal point or source from which light radiates.</note>, or in the depth, the <term>body</term> <note>original: "Corpus"; here meaning the substantial form or core of the divine light.</note> of the radiance. For the Father’s power generates the Son from eternity to eternity forevermore; if the Father were to stop generating, the Son would be nothing more. And if the Son no longer shone within the Father, the Father would be a dark valley <note>original: "finstre Thal"; likely referring to an abyss or void without light.</note>, for the Father’s power would not rise up from eternity to eternity, and the divine **Being** <note>original: "Wesen"; the fundamental essence or nature of God.</note> could not exist.

Thus the Father is the self-subsisting **Being** of all powers, and the Son is the **Heart** within the Father, who is ever-born from all the Father's powers and who, in turn, illuminates those powers. You must not think that the Son is so mixed within the Father that His person cannot be seen or recognized: no, if that were so, there would be only one person. Just as little as the sun shines *out of* the other stars—even though it has its origin from them—so little does the Son shine *out of* the powers of the Father, as far as His <term>body</term> <note>original: "corpus"; Latin for body or substance.</note> is concerned. And although

## Concerning God the Holy Spirit.

God the Holy Spirit is the third person in the triumphant holy Godhead, and proceeds from the Father and the Son. He is the holy, surging spring of joy <note>original: "Freudenquell"; a favorite term of Böhme's to describe the active, ecstatic movement of the Spirit.</note> in the entire Father, a lovely, gentle, and quiet rustling <note>original: "saussen"; referring to a whispering wind or rushing sound.</note> out of all the powers of the Father and the Son, <term>1 Kings 19:12</term> <note>original: "3. Reg. 19. v. 12."; following the older numbering where the books of Samuel and Kings were 1-4 Kings.</note> as can be seen with the prophet Elijah at Mount Horeb, and on the Day of Pentecost with the apostles of Christ, <term>Acts 2</term> <note>original: "Actor. 2."; the Book of Acts.</note>.

However, if one wishes to describe his person, substance, and property from a true foundation, one must also represent it in a likeness <note>original: "Gleichnis"; a parable or analogy.</note>; for one cannot write down the Spirit, because he is not a creature, but rather the surging power of God.

Now, look for a moment at the sun and stars—the manifold and varied stars, which are unspeakable and innumerable; these signify the Father. From those same stars the sun came to be, for God made it out of them; it signifies the Son of God. Now, from the sun and...

### Concerning the Holy Trinity.

When one now speaks or writes of three Persons in the Godhead / you must not think / that there are therefore three Gods / where each rules and governs for himself / like earthly kings upon Earth. ...a light that shines in all your **powers**, in your whole body: open the eyes of your mind and reflect upon it, and you will find it so.

   Now take note: just as the Holy Spirit proceeds from the Father and the Son, and is a self-subsisting person in the Godhead, and <term>wells</term> <note>original: "wallet"; describes a bubbling, surging, or moving motion like a spring or boiling water, representing the active movement of spirit.</note> within the whole Father: so too from the powers of your heart, veins, and brain proceeds the power that wells in your entire body. And both of these are one thing in the governance of your **mind**—your spirit—and that signifies God the Holy Spirit.    For because of this light, understanding, and governance, man is distinguished from the animals and is an angel of God, as I will clearly prove when I write about the <term>creation</term> <note>original: "erschöpffung"; here used to mean the "drawing forth" or creation of man from the divine essence.</note> of man.

   Therefore, take proper note and pay attention to the order of this book; you will find what your heart desires or ever longs for.

->Now observe:<-

In a piece of wood, a stone, or a herb there are three things / and nothing can be born or grow if among these three even one thing were to remain absent. First is the **Power** <note>original: "Krafft"; Böhme uses this to describe the essential divine energy that acts as the "father" or source of a physical body.</note>, from which a body comes / whether it be wood, stone, or herb: afterward there is within it a **Sap** <note>original: "Safft"; the vital fluid or essence, which Böhme compares to the "heart" or the "Son" in his Trinitarian analogy.</note>, which is the heart of a thing: thirdly, there is within it a welling power, scent, or taste / which is the **Spirit** of a thing / from which it grows and increases; now if one of these three is missing / then no thing can subsist.

Thus you find the likeness of the **Trinity** in the divine Being / in all things / look at whatever you will / and no one should make themselves stone-blind / and suppose it to be otherwise / or think that God has no Son and <term>Holy Spirit</term> <note>original: "H. Geist". In Böhme's system, the Father is the Power, the Son is the Sap/Light, and the Holy Spirit is the outgoing Spirit/Scent.</note>. ## Chapter 5.

### On the Corporeal Substance, Essence, and Property of an Angel.

Here the question arises: What kind of <term>body <note>original Latin: "corpus"</note></term>, form, or shape does an angel have / or how is he configured?

Just as a human is created in God’s image and likeness / so also are the angels / for they are the brothers of humans / and in the resurrection, humans will have no other form or image than the angels / as our King Christ himself testifies <margin>Matthew 22:30</margin>. Also, the angels here on earth have never revealed themselves to humans in any other shape / than the human form. Since we are to be like the angels in the resurrection / then the angels must indeed be configured as we are / otherwise we would have to take on a different image in the resurrection / which would be contrary to the first creation. Also, Moses and Elijah appeared to the disciples of Christ in their [own] form and shape on Mount Tabor <note>A reference to the Transfiguration of Jesus.</note> / though they had already been in heaven for a long time: and Elijah had been taken into heaven with a living body / and yet had no other shape now than he had on earth / <margin>2 Kings 2:11</margin>. [The body] draws [the air] in and lets it out again, for within that process lies the life of both the <term>body <note>original: *corpus*. Böhme uses this Latin term to emphasize the physical or formal structure of an entity.</note></term> and the spirit. The external <term>quality <note>original: *qualität*. This refers to the fundamental characteristics or "flavors" of nature (bitter, sweet, heat, etc.) that interact with the soul.</note></term> kindles the spirit in the heart at the first source-spring, from which all powers in the entire body are set in motion. Then that same quality rises within the bodily spirit—which is the natural spirit of an angel or a human—up into the head. There it has its princely throne and governance, and there it has its councilors by which it directs itself and acts.

The first councilor, or the first council, is the eyes. These are <term>influenced <note>original: *inficiret*. While this looks like "infected," in Böhme’s time it meant to be permeated or "stained" by the qualities of another thing, much like a dye takes to fabric.</note></term> by all things they look upon, for they are the light. Just as the light goes out from the Son of God into the whole Father, into all powers, and permeates all the powers of the Father—and in turn, all the powers of the Father permeate the light of the Son of God—so do the eyes work upon the thing they look at, and the thing works back upon the eyes. The council of the eyes then brings this before the princely throne in the head, where it is <term>evaluated <note>original: *approbiret*; to be approved or validated.</note></term>. If it pleases the spirit, the spirit brings it to the heart, and the heart gives it to the power-conduits or source-veins throughout the entire body; then the mouth, hands, and feet take action.

The second council is the ears. Their ascent also arises from all the powers in the whole body through the spirit. Their source-spring is <term>Mercury <note>original: *Marcurius*. In Böhme’s chemical-mystical vocabulary, Mercury represents the "expressed word," the spirit of sound, vibration, and the movement that brings life to the other qualities.</note></term>, or the sound that rises out of all the powers. ...nothing at all; and this <term>stirring <note>original: *rügen*. In Böhme’s unique vocabulary, this word describes a state of being moved, excited, or even "reproached" into action. But if only one limb is moved too much, it cries out to the whole body for help, and the entire body stirs itself—like a great riot, as if an enemy were present—and comes to the aid of that limb and releases it from the pain. You can see this for yourself: if you strike your finger hard, or crush or wound it—regardless of which limb it may be—the spirit at that location immediately runs to the Mother, the Heart, and complains to the Mother; and if the pain is at all significant, the Mother wakes up all the limbs of the whole body, and everything must come to the aid of that single limb.

## Chapter 6.

### How an Angel and Man are God's Likeness and Image.

Behold / as the <term>Being <note>original: *Wesen*. ## On the Mouth.

The mouth signifies that you are a **dependent** <note>original: *unallmächtiger* (literally "un-almighty"). Böhme uses this to emphasize that creatures are not self-sufficient but rely on God's power.</note> son of your Father, whether you are an angel or a human; for through the mouth you must gather your Father’s power into yourself if you wish to live. An angel must do this just as much as a human; and although he does not require the element of air in the same way a human does, he must nonetheless gather the Spirit into himself through the mouth, from which the air in this world originates. For in heaven there is no such air; rather, the qualities are entirely gentle and full of joy, like a lovely rustling; and the Holy Spirit is present among all qualities in the <term>Salniter <note>original: *Salniter*.
